#### Abstracts
##### **GRAC: Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Captioning for Surgical Scene Description**
2611.09866v1 by Rochak Dhakal, Chen Zhao, Soup Cobb, Xin Qian, Xiaofeng Yang, Weihua Zhou

Purpose: Surgical operative reports support procedural documentation, surgical training, performance assessment, and workflow review. However, extracting meaningful information often requires extensive manual review and clinical expertise. Automated surgical scene descriptions could reduce this burden by leveraging recurring surgical activities and prior knowledge.
  Methods: We propose GRAC (Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Captioning), a framework that represents surgical scenes as graphs capturing relationships among instruments, actions, and anatomical structures. Given an input scene graph, GRAC learns to retrieve relevant graph-caption pairs from training data. It compares instrument-tissue interactions between retrieved examples and the input scene, using comparison features to condition retrieved-caption representations during generation.
  Results: On the EndoVis-18 benchmark, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.88, compared with 4.23 for SGT and 3.36 for the strongest conventional Transformer baseline. Exact instrument-action-target triplet-set accuracy reached 52.6%, compared with 32.7% for SGT and 26.2% for the strongest conventional baseline. These results demonstrate improvements in language quality and factual agreement. On TORS, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.06, compared with 3.80 for the strongest baseline.
  Conclusion: GRAC demonstrates that combining structured surgical scene representations with retrieved examples improves surgical scene captioning and produces more contextually meaningful descriptions.

摘要：

##### **Can Agentic Systems Close the Gap in Radiology with Production AI Tools?**
2611.09836v1 by Aubin Charley, Julien Khlaut, Charles Corbière, Pierre Manceron, Corentin Dancette, Léo Machado

Radiology AI has developed along two paths: specialized models that perform well on narrow tasks but cover few clinical findings, and generalist models that address a broader range of tasks but remain prone to hallucination and inaccurate measurements. Combining the two through agentic systems offers a route towards comprehensive radiology reporting, but published systems are tied to one modality or body part. We describe a radiology agentic framework that standardizes how an orchestrator calls modular tools to produce a structured report, and use it to measure what such a system can do with the tools from FDA-cleared radiology AI devices. Report generation is simulated on a large multi-modality report corpus, with the orchestrator given either a catalog of finding classes consolidated from the cleared devices, each available on the modalities it is cleared for, or an unlimited catalog in which any class can be requested, and planning either from the clinical indication or from the target report. Tool outputs are simulated from the reference report, so the comparison isolates tool availability and orchestration from tool error. With the cleared catalog and the indication alone, the orchestrator recovers under a third of the clinically actionable findings; recovery improves substantially with the unlimited catalog, and further when the plan is written from the target report. Attributing every missed finding to a cause shows that most are classes no cleared device offers for the study's modality, and that a third are classes or tools that were offered and not called. Adding the most frequently missing tools closes most of the gap to the unlimited catalog on held-out reports, whereas a smaller orchestrator with the unlimited catalog gives up the whole gain. Tool availability and effective orchestration are thus complementary constraints on comprehensive AI-assisted radiology reporting.

##### **ICare-DT: a digital twin for continuous forecasting of intensive care patients**
2611.09712v1 by Yanke Li, Yahang Qi, Yumi Kim, Diego Paez-Granados

##### **Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments for Test-Time Selection**
2611.08640v1 by Hannah Guan, Yilun Du

Scaling test-time compute has proven highly effective at improving LLM reasoning. A popular method is best-of-n sampling, where a model generates many candidate solutions and one is selected. This motivates the question: how can we select the best candidate? When humans make choices, we find it more natural to compare two options than judge each in isolation. Additionally, when the options become complex, we like to judge the options one aspect at a time, such as comparing two job candidates on experience, communication, etc. In the test-time setting, candidate solutions should be judged in the same way. Here we introduce Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments (APT), which verifies by comparison and compares by aspect. Each matchup of a single elimination tournament is decided by a vote over multiple off-the-shelf aspect verifiers, each expressing a pairwise preference along its own aspect. Across five domains and three generator LLMs, APT improves over binary multi-aspect approval, self-consistency and reward model verification, with the largest gains on open-ended tasks. We find that both components matter; neither binary approval over the same aspects nor a generic pairwise judge at a matched budget matches APT. Because aspects are specified in natural language, APT also lets users steer selection toward properties they care about, better aligning with user preferences without needing to regenerate solutions. No component is trained, so APT transfers to new domains by simply rewriting prompts.

To build a general-purpose foundation model for inertial sensing, we adapt the DINO self-supervised learning (SSL) paradigm to inertial data through a set of semantics-preserving motion transformations that simulate variations in motion pattern and sensor setup. For model architecture, we use a plain transformer encoder with frequency-aware positional encoding that natively supports varying input length and sampling rate. This simple setup proved remarkably effective for learning global and dense features generalizable across users, sensor setups, tasks, and, in the case of robot odometry, embodiments. We hope IMUDINO can serve as a backbone for inertial sensing and kickstart the development of general-purpose inertial FMs.

Avoiding such contention requires careful configuration across library boundaries. Existing autotuners do not directly investigate what resources are shared between libraries, which runtime signals correlate with interference. SysPilot synthesizes contracts from documentation and code artifacts, composes contracts from the libraries used by an application, derives resource-conflict hypotheses, profiles the application to validate those hypotheses, and selects candidate configurations. 2611.04645v1 by Qijian Zheng, Wanqing Lin, Yuanfang Wang, Yijun Ge, Jordan Bate, Katie Aafjes-van Doorn, Feng Liu

Facilitative Interpersonal Skills (FIS) is a performance-based assessment method that provides a structured framework for evaluating the interpersonal competencies of mental health professionals. However, existing FIS assessment relies heavily on human observer ratings, making the evaluation process labor-intensive, costly, and difficult to scale. Progress toward automated FIS assessment has also been constrained by the lack of publicly available professional data in psychotherapy settings. To address this limitation, we introduce Therapy-FIS, a dataset for automated FIS assessment in standardized psychotherapy tasks. To our knowledge, Therapy-FIS is the first publicly available dataset in this domain. It contains facial, acoustic, and textual signals derived from standardized therapist responses to standardized mock patient stimulus clips, based on 890 therapist video recordings and 8 patient videos, and is rated with eight FIS subscale ratings and an overall FIS score. Built on Therapy-FIS, we further establish a benchmark with two downstream tasks: Therapist Performance Rating and Joint Therapist--mock-Patient Performance Rating. We also introduce a competitive baseline, DyadicFIS, with gated fusion and routed readout under therapist-only and dyadic settings. DyadicFIS obtains the best macro CCC(Concordance Correlation Coefficient) scores on both tasks (0.2807 and 0.3299). Therapy-FIS offers a reproducible resource for data-driven automated assessment of therapeutic interpersonal skills in mental health.

##### **Polyp Matching Using Deep Learning**
2611.04624v1 by Kristian Dalsbø Hindberg, Puneet Sharma, Benedicte Schelde-Olesen, Ulrik Deding, Jakob Frederik Frøkjær Justsen, Jan-Matthias Braun, Esmaeil S. Nadimi

A key challenge in colon capsule endoscopy (CCE) analysis is accurately identifying whether multiple images depict the same polyp, which is critical to avoid over-counting and unnecessary clinical interventions. Additionally, this prevents prolonged follow-up colonoscopies where endoscopists search for duplicate polyps without precise localization. This study presents a novel contrastive learning-based approach to polyp matching using a Siamese neural network architecture. The model was trained on a dataset from the Danish CareForColon2015 trial. The dataset comprises 5,103 image pairs of confirmed same polyp and 5,103 pairs of different polyps, annotated by experienced clinicians. The proposed model employs ResNet variants (ResNet18, ResNet34, ResNet50), DenseNet121 and EfficientNet B0/B3 as backbones and utilizes contrastive learning with cosine vector distance metric for classification. Experimental results demonstrate high classification accuracies of up to 92% and AUC values close to 0.98. These results suggest that automated polyp matching has the potential to reduce polyp over-counting, improve patient risk stratification, and reduce unnecessary follow-up colonoscopies.

##### **Reciprocity Can Erase Direction:tructural Boundaries and Controlled Utility of Magnetic Graph Networks**
2611.04295v1 by Dawei Han, Zicheng Wang, Yaoxing Ren, Yi Lv, Rongqian Wu, Fumin Wang

Magnetic graph networks encode direction through phase, but a learned graph can erase the intended directional mechanism before prediction. We characterize this failure for support-based magnetic Laplacians. Exact identities relate imaginary energy and distance from zero phase to amplitude-weighted reciprocity. Top-$k$ selection forces complete collapse when the observed-node count satisfies $m\leq k+1$, and dense support imposes a lower bound on reciprocal-arc fraction. We then construct paired propagation examples with identical node features and symmetric amplitudes but opposite labels, giving a provable chance-level limit for direction-erasing representations. In 72 fixed neural fits, support phase achieved test AUROC 1.0000 on a unilateral ring but 0.5000 on reciprocal ring and complete supports; continuous score phase and a real directed comparator achieved 1.0000 throughout. All models solved the direction-free control. These are oracle-direction mechanism tests, not evidence of learned clinical direction. In the preserved public DeepDRiD development study, continuous phase was active but its robustness difference from zero phase was only $+0.0036$ (descriptive 95\% interval $-0.0014$ to $+0.0095$) on validation patients used for checkpoint selection. The contribution is a structural and experimental framework for distinguishing erased direction from unestablished task utility. It provides a precise circumstance in which magnetic phase preserves useful information, without asserting universal or clinical superiority.

##### **GlucoAgent: Personalized Glucose Forecasting from Multimodal Personal Context**
2611.04126v1 by Xingzhi Sun, Hyo Jin Kim, Zhiyuan Wang, Hao Chen

Continuous glucose monitors, smartwatches, meal logs, and health profiles form a rich multimodal personal context that can significantly enhance glucose forecasting. We present GlucoAgent, an LLM-based agentic system that addresses this challenge through dynamic, case-by-case analysis at inference time. 2611.04101v1 by Jash Sayani, Gaurang Karwande, Achinth Bharadwaj

Longitudinal clinical context is often distributed across noisy, repetitive forms and narrative records. We created 30 fully synthetic cases spanning twelve clinical domains, paired each with synthetically generated structured memory, and evaluated 120 typed questions with Jev. The raw-vignette arm achieved 90.0% accuracy (95% normal-approximation confidence interval: 84.6%-95.4%); adding memory achieved 93.3% (88.9%-97.8%). Scientific discovery builds on accurate observations interpreted through domain knowledge and reasoning. To assess these foundational capabilities, we introduce ScienceVision, a cross-domain benchmark for scientific 3D understanding and reasoning through expert-level visual question answering (VQA). ##### **Measure Before You Repair: An Explainable Agent for Blind Restoration of Multi-Artifact 3-D MRI**
2611.03996v1 by Necati Sefercioglu, Mehmet Ozan Unal, Metin Ertas, Altan Cakir, Isa Yildirim

A brain MRI scan can carry an unknown mix of artifacts, so restoration has to be blind, yet most deep-learning methods are built to correct one known artifact. We present Dx-Rx, an agent in which a multimodal large language model (MLLM) measures suspected artifacts before it repairs them. To our knowledge, it is the first agent to restore whole 3-D MRI volumes, whereas earlier MRI agents restore 2-D slices. In a loop, it looks at slices of the 3-D volume, calls five diagnostic tools (noise, spike, ghosting, bias field, motion) and four restoration tools in any order it chooses, checks each repair, can return to any earlier version to change the order, and ends with a radiology-style report of its diagnostic reasons, the repairs it applied and their results. We simulated up to five artifacts on 148 whole 3-D T1-weighted volumes, at levels calibrated to radiologists' grades of real scans. An MLLM that only looked at the image, as earlier MRI agents do, removed at most 19.7 % of the artifact error. With the best of five MLLMs, Dx-Rx removed a mean 64.5 %, nearly matching the 64.6 % of the same tools given the ground-truth artifacts, which are never known for a clinical scan. With all five models, it made no volume worse and was on par with or better than an expert rule-based pipeline built from the same tools (62.3 %). Code and demo are public at https://itu-biai.github.io/measure-before-you-repair.

Overall, the results show that CoT loss weighting should be matched to the task format and the knowledge already encoded in the base model, rather than using a single fine-tuning strategy for all clinical QA tasks.

##### **Rethinking Next-Visit Diagnosis Prediction Beyond Aggregate Metrics: Retrieval--Memory Multi-Agent Adjudication**
2611.03826v1 by Heejoon Koo

Next-visit diagnosis prediction forecasts the diagnoses documented at a patient's next encounter, turning retrospective electronic health records into prospective clinical prediction. However, conventional aggregate evaluation obscures its multifaceted nature: distributionally, models must handle variable-size diagnosis sets and long-tailed labels; temporally, they must distinguish persistent, newly recorded, and recurrent diagnoses; and both vary with observed history depth. We introduce an evaluation framework along these dimensions and apply it to rule-based heuristics, LLM-based prompting, retrieval, memory, and multi-agent approaches. This reveals that repeating the last visit outperforms most LLM-based approaches overall despite predicting no new or recurrent diagnoses, and that retrieval and memory exhibit distinct profiles whose relative strengths vary across temporal transitions and backbones. Motivated by this, we propose LONGINUS, a retrieval-memory multi-agent adjudication framework combining evidence retrieved from patient records with structured memory of the patient trajectory. Using cross-view agreement as a reliability heuristic, it preserves shared predictions and adjudicates disagreements without revealing their source. Across MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV with four LLM backbones, LONGINUS achieves the best transition-specific performance among LLM-based methods in nearly all settings while remaining competitive with them on aggregate metrics.

Conventional domain adaptation methods address domain shift but require access to source data during adaptation, which is often infeasible due to privacy regulations. Multi-source-free unsupervised domain adaptation (MSFDA) addresses this limitation by leveraging multiple labeled source domains to generalize to an unlabeled target domain without requiring access to source data. However, existing MSFDA methods rely on full backbone fine-tuning, leading to catastrophic forgetting and overfitting on small clinical datasets, and address domain shift at the aggregation stage without establishing a shared domain-invariant feature space. Furthermore, their reliance on hard pseudo-labels or confidence-weighted aggregation introduces noisy supervision signals under domain shift. To address these limitations, we propose CAT-CKD, consisting of two components: (1) contrastive adapter training (CAT), which trains lightweight ConvPass adapters within a frozen ViT backbone using supervised contrastive learning (SCL) to establish a shared domain-invariant feature space before source-specific model training, and (2) consensus knowledge distillation (CKD), which aggregates logits from multiple source models into a consensus supervisory signal and adapts a student model on unlabeled target data using KL divergence. Experiments on five publicly available skin lesion datasets show that CAT-CKD achieves an average AUROC of 86.1%, outperforming existing MSFDA methods while requiring only 4.3M trainable parameters. However, the data infrastructure and computational resources that provide the foundation for AI workflows in ecology and biodiversity science remain fragmented. Moreover, the flow of value remains largely unidirectional: data move from collectors, through repositories and aggregators into models, and finally to research products or management actions. The derived value, such as corrected taxonomies, annotations, quality flags, and insights, rarely returns to source communities. We propose an extension of the original FAIR principles to address the needs of AI-ready infrastructure: the AI-FAIR metadata criteria, which place AI-enabled science in the context of a bidirectional Question-Data-Model triangle. This framework is designed to facilitate large-scale content-based analysis across diverse collections and ensure that the scientists generating the data benefit from the AI-enabled advances built on their data. Alongside these criteria, we introduce an agentic evaluation workflow that both rates existing datasets on the AI-FAIR and traditional FAIR criteria and provides actionable feedback where critical metadata is missing or incomplete. This modular dataset evaluation tool, paired with a domain-appropriate checklist, translates these principles into a reusable assessment of machine-learning readiness, AI-readiness for specified tasks, traceability, and ethical and CARE-aligned stewardship. ##### **CAREFUL-Bench: Effect-Aware, Judge-Free Evaluation of Multi-Turn LLM Function Calling**
2611.02978v2 by Alexander Kovalenko, Artem Redchych

The multi-turn checker of the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard (BFCL) checks only the final state of a simulated service, never the calls that produced it. Extra calls cost nothing, no function doc says what a function does, and the scripted user never answers a proposal, so a model that asks before deleting or paying fails the turn; 322 of the 734 reference turns in the base category act destructively without confirmation. We add effect information to BFCL without replacing it. Effect declarations for all 128 functions are inferred by executing each function in BFCL's own simulators and diffing the state; the remaining judgment, which writes are destructive, is made by four annotators, two humans and two language models (Fleiss' kappa = 0.75), and adjudicated. A judge-free replay scorer re-scores any logged BFCL run, reproduces BFCL's results on all 800 reference trajectories, and reports, alongside the official score, unrequested destructive calls, duplicate writes, redundant reads, surplus steps and their token cost. A confirmation-aware protocol adds a deterministic user that answers proposals. On 11 open models the effect-aware score preserves the official ranking (rho = 0.99) but changes the gaps: the leader loses 14.0 points to unrequested destructive calls, and surplus steps account for 2-28% of all input tokens. Effect hints in the function docs produce no measurable change for any of eight models, consistent with their advisory status in the Model Context Protocol. A user that answers recovers up to four points for the models that ask and none for the rest, with every comparison paired on the same conversations. The gains come from clarifying questions rather than requests for permission: of the 712 conversations that require a destructive call, no model completes more than 6 with every such call confirmed, a difference the official score does not record.

In this study, we demonstrate how practitioners can estimate energy consumption and arrive at reliable emission estimates for medical QA tasks available in the OpenMedical-LLM leaderboard by investigating 13 LLMs from 3 model families ranging from 270M to 14B parameters. Promotion requires a combined simulation-and-inspection score at least at parity with the deployed version, confirmation through an online comparison with the incumbent, and human approval.

##### **Measuring AI Agents as Teammates with Collaborative Intelligence**

Yet teams of AI agents are starting to make breakthroughs and discoveries in science, medicine, mathematics and software engineering. As AI begins to work in teams of agents and people, the ability to collaborate becomes a core part of their intelligence. Research on human teams has measured this ability for decades. Inspired by this, we introduce the Collaborative Intelligence Benchmark, which measures how well an AI agent works as a teammate. It contains 240 tasks across 8 dimensions of teamwork, from refusing an unsafe shortcut to checking the work that a weaker model leaves behind. Each task comes from a real record or an existing benchmark, from aviation accident reports and clinical cases to coding tasks and negotiations, and has a programmatically verifiable outcome. To isolate teamwork from individual capability, models are scored only on tasks that they can solve by themselves when they have full information. We evaluate 18 closed- and open-weight models and find that every model, across sizes and families, has clear weaknesses as a teammate, with the best model scoring only 82.2 out of 100. Frontier models almost always refuse unsafe shortcuts and report misconduct, yet they cave under interpersonal pressure, reaching the best overall deal in only 14% of conflicts, and their pass rate drops from 97% to 81% when they take over a task in which a weaker model has cheated. Crucially, general capability does not guarantee good teamwork, as models with nearly the same capability score differ by up to 11 points as teammates. ##### **When Prediction Shapes the Future: A BlueSky Agenda for Reflexive Time Series Analysis**
2611.02511v1 by Tao Xie, Yiqun Zhang, Yiu-ming Cheung

Time-series models are increasingly deployed in operational systems (e.g., inventory management and clinical care) where predictions trigger interventions that alter future observations, producing records we term AI-shaped trajectories. This sequence-prediction coupling deepens as such systems evolve into agents that autonomously predict, act, and remember across long horizons. Such AI-shaped trajectories support immediate performance monitoring, but turn ambiguous once reused as passive data. An algorithm may then misread a prediction-prevented stockout as naturally stable demand, or an averted medical crisis as a false alarm. This ambiguity then propagates into everything built on the record, from retraining and agent memory to downstream predictive tasks. As a result, systems may internalize these intervention effects as natural phenomena, drifting further from the truth at every closed-loop cycle. In this Blue Sky paper, we advocate an agenda for reflexive time-series analysis that shifts the analytical object from the intervention-agnostic sequence to the prediction-action-observation loop. The agenda requires closed-loop provenance to track the operational context (e.g., predictions, policies, and actions), sequence identification to estimate how those actions affect future values under explicit causal assumptions, and closed-loop evaluation to separate intrinsic process prediction from policy-driven effects. This foundation is essential for integrating autonomous agents into such loops at scale, enabling the joint learning of intervention effects and intrinsic dynamics for robust temporal representations.

Across five 2D tasks, we evaluate 11 public pretrained encoders (a controlled sample, not a census) using frozen features and separately trained $1\times 1$ readouts with four seeds per encoder. An expanded 17-encoder analysis shows that distinct checkpoints within the same encoder family remain more correlated than cross-family pairs. At the pooled 33rd-percentile Dice threshold, four-member pools drawn from distinct encoders have mean effective sizes of 1.56-2.92, estimated from failure correlations in pools with enough failures and passes per member. In the primary pool, richer readouts and fine-tuning narrow the Dice-correlation gap between pairs sharing a checkpoint (or the DINOv2-B/S release) and all other pairs. Yet dependence between different encoders can increase, and fine-tuning does not consistently increase effective pool size at 33rd-percentile thresholds recomputed for each training recipe. A smaller between-group gap can coexist with greater dependence. Pool evaluation should therefore report absolute dependence and threshold-specific effective size alongside accuracy and nominal pool size. The CoFailMed release provides aligned per-case traces, analysis scripts and study code to reproduce the central audit results.

##### **Clinical-Protocol-Aware Cyber Resilience for Connected Clinical Technologies Under AI-Enabled Adversarial Conditions**
2610.41784v1 by Pranav Patel, Siva Kannathasan, Prerak Patel, Jigar Patel, Vansh Patel, Sheel Patel

Healthcare increasingly relies on connected medical devices, clinical information systems, cloud services, and distributed-care technologies, creating cybersecurity risks across clinical workflows. AI-enabled capabilities can accelerate vulnerability discovery and attack-path analysis, increasing the need to limit compromise propagation. This paper proposes a clinical-protocol-aware cyber-resilience framework based on bounded clinical micro-networks and controlled interconnections. The framework integrates clinical factors into cybersecurity design, management, and evaluation, covering prevention, containment, and incident response. A parameterized Monte Carlo simulation distinguishes technical cyber exposure from modeled clinical consequence. Experiments investigate architectural connectivity, adversarial capability, security controls, protocol dependency, risk alignment, communication directionality, lifecycle integration, and response timing. Under the simulation assumptions, connected architecture increases mean cyber exposure by 56.3% relative to segmented architecture, while combined prevention and containment reduce exposure by 60.1% relative to no controls. Increasing protocol dependency raises the modeled clinical-impact index from 1.021 to 7.567 without changing mean cyber exposure, demonstrating that technical exposure and clinical consequence can diverge. Risk-alignment experiments further indicate higher modeled clinical impact when greater technical risk corresponds to greater clinical significance. The findings support comparative architectural analysis, not calibrated estimates of real-world cyber risk, control effectiveness, or patient harm. The framework provides a clinical-context-aware approach to security-boundary design, control prioritization, and coordinated containment in connected healthcare environments.

##### **Advancing Clinical Trial Outcome Prediction with Domain-Specific Large Language Models**
2610.41710v1 by Burak Bakkar, Hana Moalla, Mohamed Salem Jedidi, Haifa Ben Messaoud, Nicolas Lopez Carranza

Clinical trial failures are a major barrier to efficient drug development, incurring substantial costs and delaying patient access to effective therapies. Structured approaches such as the Hierarchical Interaction Network (HINT) predict trial outcomes by explicitly modelling interactions between drugs, diseases and protocols, but make limited use of the free text in trial registrations. We evaluate two domain-specific language models, BioBERT (108M parameters) and BioMistral (7B parameters), for phase-level outcome prediction on the TOP benchmark (12,477 trials), representing each trial as text built from ten ClinicalTrials.gov fields. We compare fine-tuning (full fine-tuning for BioBERT, low-rank adaptation for BioMistral) with probing, in which the pretrained model is frozen and only a lightweight classifier is trained on its embeddings. On the held-out test set of each phase, all four language-model variants achieved higher ROC-AUC (0.613--0.772) and PR-AUC (0.649--0.901) than the published HINT results, corresponding to relative PR-AUC gains of 10--20\%. F1 differences were small and mixed, and no model, including HINT, clearly exceeded the F1 of a trivial classifier that predicts success for every trial. Under the configurations tested, probing performed comparably to fine-tuning: fine-tuned models scored highest on ROC-AUC and PR-AUC in Phases I and II, and frozen BioMistral embeddings in Phase III (ROC-AUC 0.772; PR-AUC 0.901), although most differences between variants were below 0.03 and were not tested for significance. These results suggest that pretrained biomedical representations already encode signal relevant to trial outcome, and that probing is a computationally cheaper alternative to fine-tuning for this task.

##### **RECITE: Revising Eligibility Criteria Incorporating Textual Evidence**
2610.41368v1 by Russell Ro, Reza Abbasi-Asl

Overly restrictive eligibility criteria contribute to widespread enrollment failure in clinical trials, yet published recommendations for how specific criteria should be broadened remain disconnected from trial design at scale. We introduce RECITE (Revising Eligibility Criteria Incorporating Textual Evidence), a framework for automated discovery, extraction, and implementation of evidence-based eligibility criteria revisions. RECITE uses protocol amendment documents from ClinicalTrials.gov to construct a dataset of 11,913 curated instances across 5,735 trials, each pairing original criteria with documentary evidence and target revisions. We benchmark fifteen LLM configurations under truncated-to-context-window and retrieval-augmented generation conditions, on a 3,116-instance sample under an LLM-as-judge protocol validated against ten domain experts (judge vs. human quadratic-weighted Cohen's $κ_{qw} = 0.694 \pm 0.096$). The best open-weight model, Gemma 2 9B, achieves $κ_{qw}$-aligned ordinal score $3.0 \pm 0.9$ on a 0--4 scale (normalized 0.742) and the best closed-weight model, GPT-4o, reaches $3.0 \pm 0.8$ (normalized 0.745). Using an agentic literature discovery system, we then identified 44 paper--trial matches across five publications providing trial-design and EC-modification evidence. Our findings suggest that existing clinical knowledge can be operationalized into actionable trial design recommendations.

摘要：

##### **Confident Fools or Careful Searchers? A Behavioural Study of When LLMs Search Beyond Their Static Internal Memory**
2610.41124v1 by Nipun D. Pathirage, Kwan Srijomkwan

Agentic LLM assistants must repeatedly decide whether to answer from memory or to call a tool such as web search. Unnecessary searches cost latency and money; missing ones yield confident but unsupported answers. Prior work mostly measures verbalised confidence or trains new adaptive-retrieval methods, and rarely audits the search decisions that deployed products actually make. We introduce a benchmark of 400 multiple-choice questions that combines widely known inside-knowledge (IK) questions, which should be answered directly, with outside-knowledge (OK) questions about facts from news published after the models' training cut-offs (to our knowledge), which should trigger search or, without tools, abstention. Each item offers an abstain option, and two action-level scores, NAS and KIS, reward searching on OK questions and restraint on IK questions. Across five commercial LLMs with and without their vendors' web search, GPT-5 mini and Grok 4 Fast search on over 90% of OK but only 1% of IK questions, whereas Mistral Small searches on just 20% of OK questions, often abstaining instead of using its tool; one product's search indicator proved unusable. Searching is partly triggered by explicit temporal cues: on uncued OK questions, the NAS of the two selective models falls from about 0.72 to 0.57--0.58. The scores thus measure the search triggering of specific products, not metacognition in a strict sense.

##### **BABA-Bench: Evaluating Long-Horizon Patient-Advocacy Agents**
2610.41076v1 by Shriram Vasudevan, Videet Mehta, Connor Sweeney

An LLM agent that helps a patient get care must do two things. It must reach the correct outcome, and it must follow the necessary procedure. For example, the agent must get the patient's permission before it shares the patient's records. We introduce BABA-Bench, a benchmark of 47 interactive worlds. We built each world from a de-identified, real patient advocacy case that expert advocates reviewed. In each world, the agent must find hidden information and coordinate insurers, clinics, suppliers, pharmacies, and community organizations. The engine checks each action at the time the agent does it. It checks if the agent has permission, if the agent has evidence for the action, and if the action is prohibited. We evaluated 14 frontier models with three runs on each world, for a total of 1,974 runs. Agents often reached the correct outcome by an invalid route. Of the 921 runs that reached all required outcomes, 254 (28%) did not follow the necessary procedure. Claude Opus 5 reached all required outcomes in 81.6% of its runs, but only 52.5% of its runs passed all checks. When we gave two models a verified route, their pass rates increased from 29.8% to 91.5% and from 44.0% to 97.2%. For these models, the main problem is to find a route, not to execute it.

##### **Towards Understanding Score-Seeking in LLMs**
2610.41014v1 by Aghyad Deeb, Ryan Greenblatt, Alex Mallen

Production large language models sometimes reward-hack, exploiting flaws in how tasks are scored rather than performing the tasks as intended, sometimes in highly sophisticated and adversarial ways. We investigate the motivations driving this behavior in GPT 5.6 Sol, GPT 5.5, and a checkpoint of OpenAI o3 without safety training ("exp-rl-cap-late") by observing model behavior in agentic environments where the grader is leaked. In our environments, the models appear to be score-seeking: trying to score well according to graders that they believe assess their current actions, even at the expense of violating explicit user instructions. They appear to care about score itself rather than its downstream consequences, violating instructions at approximately the same rate whether the score is indicated to be used for training, for evaluation, or not at all. We observe evidence that the more recent models care to some extent about scores that do not assess their own current actions. GPT 5.6 Sol and GPT 5.5 help other agents 24.6 percentage points more often on average when the other agent stands to gain a score of 1 rather than 0 from being helped. However, when grading or monitoring another trajectory, we find models never collude by distorting its score.

##### **Can Personal Requirements Become Supervision for Planning?**
2610.40992v1 by Junwei Wu, Runze Yan, Yuzhang Xie, Hanqi Luo, Ziwei Dong, Jiaying Lu, Xiao Hu, Carl Yang

Managing multiple chronic conditions through diet requires satisfying disease- and medication-specific requirements jointly across meals and days while preserving established eating habits. These requirements are public in clinical guidelines and drug labels, yet turning them into a feasible plan for one person remains difficult for language-model agents. In our dietitian-approved weekly planning evaluation built from NHANES, thirteen models, from medically fine-tuned 8B models to GPT-6.1 Sol, pass fewer than 3% of complete plans through direct generation. Explicit rules, higher reasoning effort, or model-directed tools leave GPT-6.1 Sol at no more than 8%. We present Noura, which pairs LLM planning with executable personal specifications instantiated from public clinical guidance and individual health and dietary profiles. During planning, the specification grounds foods, optimizes portions, checks the complete multi-day plan, and guides revision. Our key insight is that a specification that rejects a plan can also determine what to learn from it. Noura turns executed plans and contextually credited revisions into supervision for compact models, recovering targets even from trajectories that never achieve complete success. The same specification supplies supervised targets, preferences, and rewards for post-training. On 4,993 held-out participants, Noura-SFT raises Qwen3-14B's complete-plan pass rate from 24.0% to 72.5% within the same execution loop, cuts rule violations by 97.5% with no observed critical failures, and improves dietary quality. The locally hosted 14B planner incurs fewer rule violations than its GPT-6 Luna teacher. A matched comparison shows that executed targets reduce rule violations by 41% over model-written proposals. Across four Qwen, Phi, and Llama backbones, self-training alone reduces rule violations by 41-61% on a 1,249-person population subset.

##### **Nedimo: An Evidence-Grounded Multilingual Pipeline for AI-Generated Sleep Stories**
2610.40970v1 by Georgios Makridis, Stefanos Kampouris

Generative audio systems can produce large libraries of personalized bedtime content, but ordinary media objectives (retention, completion, and novelty) are poorly aligned with the goal of supporting a transition toward sleep. Direct evidence for narrated bedtime audio in adults is mixed, and a recent active-controlled trial did not support such audio as a standalone treatment for sleep disturbance. We translate this uncertain evidence into conservative engineering requirements and present a multilingual pipeline for generating and narrating low-arousal sleep stories. The implemented prototype separates deterministic planning from large language model generation, represents content and delivery parameters explicitly, shapes each script through a four-phase attention taper, validates structural and lexical constraints, performs a bounded error-aware retry, and quarantines unresolved outputs. Provider-agnostic text-to-speech synthesis is followed by paragraph-aware joining, two-pass loudness normalization, and a publication gate that re-measures the saved, encoded file against fixed loudness, loudness-range, and true-peak limits. The product data model records stop position, replay, early abandonment, and completion while treating these events as ambiguous behavioral proxies rather than clinical outcomes. We contribute an evidence-to-requirement mapping, a traceable guardrail architecture with explicit acceptance thresholds, technical evaluation criteria, and a prospective protocol for testing sleep-onset effects. No clinical efficacy is claimed; the system is positioned as a relaxation and attentional-support tool whose benefit and possible reliance must be evaluated empirically.

##### **CAMEL: Cross-Architecture Masked Embedding Learning for Ultra-Lightweight Retinal Foundation Models**
2610.40954v1 by Andre Guarnier De Mitri, Giovani Decico Lucafo, Miller Matheus Lima Anacleto Rocha, Diego Furtado Silva

Recent progress in deep learning has substantially advanced automated grading of ophthalmologic pathologies, supported by large annotated datasets and improved representation learning. However, a significant gap remains between state-of-the-art performance and clinical scalability. In particular, foundation models have reshaped image-based medical screening and diagnosis. These large-scale architectures trained on extensive datasets learn rich, generalizable feature representations that can be adapted to a wide range of downstream tasks. In ophthalmology, RETFound, one of the most relevant foundation models for retinal images, performs strongly across classification, segmentation, and risk prediction tasks. Despite their effectiveness, foundation models are often computationally prohibitive, hindering their deployment in real-world clinical environments where hardware constraints and latency are critical. To bridge this gap, we introduce CAMEL (Cross-Architecture Masked Embedding Learning), a novel cross-architecture distillation framework that uses an asymmetric masking strategy during embedding learning to effectively transfer knowledge from massive teacher foundation models to compact, lightweight student models. Our experimental evaluation across four datasets shows that CAMEL preserves accuracy while drastically reducing computational complexity, enabling lightweight yet highly accurate models suitable for resource-limited clinical settings.

##### **NECA: Neighbor Event Curve Augmentation for Multi-Horizon ICU Deterioration Prediction**
2610.40420v2 by Chenyang Wang, Theresa Jiawei Tang, Xinran Liu, Xinyu Lu, Xulei Yang

Early prediction of ICU organ dysfunction events supports timely clinical monitoring and intervention, but estimating short-term organ-specific risk from early ICU records remains challenging due to irregular measurements, heterogeneous clinical variables, and the need to predict risk across multiple future horizons. Existing supervised models usually treat each patient as an isolated sample and do not explicitly use the observed event timing of similar historical patients. We propose Neighbor Event-Curve Augmentation (NECA), a model-agnostic method that retrieves training-set patients with similar early-window profiles and converts their subsequent organ dysfunction event onset times into cumulative event-curve features over 6h, 12h, 24h, and 42h. These features are concatenated with the original patient representation and used by standard downstream predictors. On PhysioNet 2012 and a 4,000-stay MIMIC-IV subset, NECA improves average PR-AUC and ROC-AUC across four organ dysfunction event tasks and seven supervised learners. The largest gain appears for neurological dysfunction events, where task-level average PR-AUC increases from 0.461 to 0.557 on PhysioNet 2012. Ablations show that clinically meaningful retrieval and horizon-specific neighbor risk drive most of the improvement, while the full cumulative curve adds task-dependent temporal context. ##### **The Athlete as a Scientist of One: Agentic Bayesian Experimental Design for Individualized Physiology from Consumer Wearables**
2610.40370v1 by Behnam Moradi

Large language model (LLM) agents for personal health read wearable data and recommend, but do not learn about the individual in a way that can be audited or falsified. We present EpiCoach, an agentic system that treats one athlete, a cross-country mountain biker, as the subject of accountable N-of-1 science. Claims about the athlete live in an evidence-gated hypothesis registry: each must issue a probabilistic prediction about a future session before it occurs, and its belief moves only through computed evidence from a logged statistical fit. Predictions come from a mechanistic digital twin inferred from heart rate, GPS and barometric altitude: a lagged heart-rate response model, a fitness-fatigue model with learned time constants, and a latent technical-descending skill state, measured on discovered trail segments. The agent prescribes the next training week as a constrained experiment maximizing information gain subject to load and safety constraints, around two instrument rides. We evaluate the system two ways over eighteen months of data. A rolling-origin replay benchmark shows the twin beats standard forecasters on heart rate but not segment time, and the agentic loop matches a static model. A live prospective phase runs the weekly cycle: in its first three weeks the ledger sealed 20 predictions, scored 14, the descent forecast calibrated after one correction, the climb forecast pessimistic as the athlete improved, and the safety officer vetoed the agent twice. Every correction is preserved in the registry, a template for auditable, falsifiable personalization in digital health. We release the registry, benchmark and protocol.

##### **Radiology Guideline-derived Decision Graph for Efficient Clinical Decision Support**
2610.40327v1 by Xuewen Zhou, Zhen Chen

Radiological risk stratification and management planning are essential downstream tasks in medical imaging. Automating these tasks is challenging because clinical guideline criteria are distributed across prose, tables, and cross-referenced definitions. During compilation, a large language model partitions guidelines into section-based chunks, extracts local decision subgraphs with linked definitions, and aggregates related branches into an executable decision graph. ##### **Learned Terminal Costs for Short-Horizon Model Predictive Control of Autonomous Trucks in Interactive Highway Traffic**

Autonomous highway driving for heavy-duty trucks is a challenging control problem. The greater mass, dimensions and intricate dynamics increase the requirements for cooperation from surrounding traffic in critical maneuvers, requiring a careful balance between safety constraints and efficient long-horizon decision making. Model Predictive Control (MPC) provides rigorous constraint satisfaction but relies on terminal cost which can limit its effectiveness at short, computationally affordable horizons. Reinforcement Learning (RL) captures long-term driving behavior but offers no constraint guarantees and tends toward overly conservative policies when trained under sparse collision penalties. In this paper, we propose a hybrid MPC and RL framework for highway driving in autonomous trucks, where a finite-horizon MPC, solved via dynamic programming, uses a pre-trained RL model for terminal cost estimation. We evaluate the framework in a simulation environment with reactive surrounding traffic and compare it against pure MPC with zero terminal cost, rollout with terminal cost estimation using a base policy, and pure RL. Results show that the hybrid approach achieves zero collisions with the lowest cost of any configuration.

##### **EnvOS: Auditing Computer-Use Agents When the World Moves On : Phenomenon-Based Task Construction, Certification, and Evidence-Based Evaluation**

When an agent fails, the cause may be the agent, but it may also be a change that did not fire as intended, an update the agent never received, or a grader that scored the episode wrongly. ##### **EPC-RawDC: Equivariant Physics Constrained 3D Diffusion with Raw Data Consistency for CBCT to CT Image Synthesis**

On SynthRAD2025, projection consistency and equivariant errors were reduced by 55.0% and 41.4% relative to DDPM. These results demonstrate that combining a volumetric diffusion prior with scanner geometry and scan specific projection measurements improves quantitative CBCT to CT synthesis across both simulated and measured projection domains. #### Abstracts
##### **On Agentic Manipulation Policies**
2611.09892v1 by Chen Chen, Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, Xueting Li, Jean-Baptiste Regli, Kejia Ren, Petr Švec, David J. Yoon, Matthias Müller, Vladlen Koltun

Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6\% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2\% and 42.9\% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation.

摘要：

##### **CPU-ARC: Agentic Reasoning Caching for on-CPU Reasoning Reuse**
2611.09891v1 by Peter Belcak, Greg Heinrich, Saurav Muralidharan, Shizhe Diao, Pavlo Molchanov

Large language models reinvent the wheel all the time. They conflate task-solving with the execution of the solutions, which forces them to solve every problem from scratch. We show that once a task has been reasoned through, the application of its solution to a new problem instance can be done very efficiently and with little involvement from language models. We introduce agentic reasoning caching, a method for constructing robust CPU-executable agentic reasoning caches (ARCs) from LLM reasoning traces. ARCs show on-par performance with frontier LLMs across a range of difficult mathematical and scientific tasks, are fully explainable, continually trainable, robust to input perturbations, and 16-291x cheaper to invoke than the source model.

摘要：

##### **On the Reliability of Majority-Vote Pseudo-Labels in Test-Time Reinforcement Learning**
2611.09872v1 by Haoyu Wang, Ru Wang, Yuanyuan Wang, Jiaxian Guo, Mingming Gong

Test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL) adapts pretrained language models to unlabeled target-domain data using self-generated pseudo-labels, whose reliability remains poorly understood. We analyze majority-vote supervision for TTRL with online Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and decompose its error into population-majority bias and prompt-dependent finite-rollout variation. Because population-majority bias is generally unidentifiable without external supervision, we focus on mitigating the downstream effects of finite-rollout variation. We propose \emph{Prompt-Conditional Distributionally Robust DPO (PC-Dr.DPO)}, which conditions within-prompt robust reweighting on observed vote reliability to make updates from sampling-sensitive prompts more conservative. We further introduce a cache-based mechanism to reduce self-supervised forgetting during online adaptation. Across six mathematical and knowledge-intensive reasoning benchmarks and five model backbones, PC-Dr.DPO matches or outperforms the strongest TTRL baseline in 24 of 30 settings.

摘要：

##### **CodeQuant2: Enhanced Outlier Mitigation and Adaptive Clustering for Low-Precision LRMs**
2611.09868v1 by Xingyu Liu, Xiangyang Yin, Tianhua Xia, Haiyu Wang, Sai Qian Zhang

Large reasoning models (LRMs) improve task performance by generating longer intermediate reasoning traces, but this substantially increases inference costs and makes low-precision quantization increasingly important. However, aggressively quantized models often perform poorly on reasoning tasks. We observe larger activation fluctuations during the first decoding steps of reasoning. Calibration that omits these early states may miss their activation fluctuations. To address these reasoning-specific quantization challenges, we propose \textbf{CodeQuant2}, a clustering-based LUT quantization framework for low-precision large reasoning models. CodeQuant2 mitigates activation outliers with rotation and scaling, uses clustering to represent non-uniform weight distributions, and adapts codebook sizes to the quantization difficulty of each layer. Guided by our observation of reasoning-time activation behavior, CodeQuant2 further improves calibration sample selection to better match actual decoding statistics. Combined with our customized LUT CUDA kernel, CodeQuant2 delivers substantial accuracy improvements on reasoning benchmarks while achieving up to $2.85\times$ speedup over BF16.

摘要：

##### **GRAC: Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Captioning for Surgical Scene Description**
2611.09866v1 by Rochak Dhakal, Chen Zhao, Soup Cobb, Xin Qian, Xiaofeng Yang, Weihua Zhou

Purpose: Surgical operative reports support procedural documentation, surgical training, performance assessment, and workflow review. However, extracting meaningful information often requires extensive manual review and clinical expertise. Automated surgical scene descriptions could reduce this burden by leveraging recurring surgical activities and prior knowledge.
  Methods: We propose GRAC (Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Captioning), a framework that represents surgical scenes as graphs capturing relationships among instruments, actions, and anatomical structures. Given an input scene graph, GRAC learns to retrieve relevant graph-caption pairs from training data. It compares instrument-tissue interactions between retrieved examples and the input scene, using comparison features to condition retrieved-caption representations during generation.
  Results: On the EndoVis-18 benchmark, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.88, compared with 4.23 for SGT and 3.36 for the strongest conventional Transformer baseline. Exact instrument-action-target triplet-set accuracy reached 52.6%, compared with 32.7% for SGT and 26.2% for the strongest conventional baseline. These results demonstrate improvements in language quality and factual agreement. On TORS, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.06, compared with 3.80 for the strongest baseline.
  Conclusion: GRAC demonstrates that combining structured surgical scene representations with retrieved examples improves surgical scene captioning and produces more contextually meaningful descriptions.

摘要：

##### **Grasp-TAGS: Task-Aware Grasp Selection via Critic Distillation for Prehensile Loco-Manipulation**
2611.09864v1 by Xun Tu, Minghao Zou, Karthik Desingh

Loco-manipulation, which requires mobile manipulators to coordinate locomotion and manipulation over long horizons, is a key capability for large-object rearrangement in applications such as service robotics and warehouse automation. Despite recent progress, reliably executing these tasks remains challenging because performance depends on complex robot-object and object-environment interaction dynamics. Existing approaches address this challenge either through model-based methods, which often require accurate dynamics models, or through learning-based methods, which reduce this requirement but typically assume a fixed or predefined initial grasp. However, different geometrically stable grasps can lead to substantially different downstream manipulation performance. Therefore, we propose Grasp-TAGS, a task-aware grasp selection framework for goal-conditioned large-object rearrangement. In this setting, successful alignment should preserve both the global structure of the target distribution and the instance-level correspondence observed in paired source--target training data. Existing objectives typically address only part of this goal: contrastive losses encourage relative similarity, distribution-matching losses improve marginal agreement, and pointwise regression fits each source sample to its paired target without explicitly modeling target-space path geometry. Current evaluations of agentic AI systems primarily rely on benchmarks. However, the static nature of benchmarks makes them uninformative about the value of auditing interventions at deployment time. For reliable deployment, we often need to determine within an agent's trajectory whether or not to intervene, e.g., by verifying the agent's current output or steering it toward different behavior, using either human review or more costly models or time-consuming agentic supervision. The value of such interventions is fundamentally a causal inference question, asking whether the causal effect of such an intervention is beneficial for the final outcome. Estimating this causal effect is particularly challenging due to the dynamic nature of the problem: the impact of an intervention depends on the current context and environment, as well as the agent's future behavior. We develop the first methodologically grounded framework for evaluating the causal effects of randomized interventions within agent trajectories. By leveraging a relatively small dataset of agent trajectories collected under randomized interventions such as verification or screening, we can estimate the dynamic causal effect of a rich class of (contextual) intervention policies. The structural parameters that we estimate can also be used to construct safety leaderboards that capture the risk of various agentic actions, which can be used by practitioners for audit prioritization. Applying our methods to tau-bench Retail, ToolSandbox, and ImpossibleBench, we find that intervention effects vary substantially across agent actions and states. More broadly, our work suggests a rich research agenda at the intersection of causal inference and agentic evaluation, which we believe is essential for the reliable and safe deployment of AI agents in the real world.

RidgeFill shortens prefill by not computing most of a model's upper layers for the prompt. RidgeFill retrofits it onto models that were never trained for it: the map is a ridge regression fitted once in closed form from one pass over calibration text, the layer's own projections run unchanged, and a chosen set of rows, the last few thousand tokens, and every decoding step run the full model. Sparse attention addresses this bottleneck by restricting each query to a subset of the context, but its effectiveness hinges on identifying relevant regions both cheaply and reliably. Existing training-free methods primarily rely on runtime relevance estimates, with selection dimensionality and granularity typically governed by fixed heuristics rather than the intrinsic interaction structure of pretrained attention heads. Building on this insight, we propose Head-Adaptive Sparse Attention (HASA), a training-free sparse attention framework that jointly adapts selection resolution along these two dimensions. Along the feature dimension, the singular spectrum of each head's query-key operator determines an effective rank, yielding a compact, head-specific response space. Along the sequence dimension, neighboring regions are progressively merged according to the directional coherence of their token responses in this same space, while heterogeneous regions remain separate. ##### **Verifier Noise Is a Temperature: Testing Tilt Predictions in Finite-Budget LLM-Guided Search**
2611.09699v1 by Xiaomeng Li

LLM-guided search loops propose a modified candidate, score it, and keep it if it scores better. When both candidates are re-scored at every comparison, as in the minibatch acceptance gates of prompt optimizers, keep-if-better is a reversible Markov chain if the score difference is logistic and the mutation operator is reversible, and its stationary law is the mutation equilibrium tilted by exp(beta f). For any continuous symmetric noise difference the same tilt is the stationary law of a small-step diffusion limit, with beta = 4F'(0); for a minibatch gate beta grows with the square root of the minibatch size. Caching the incumbent's score instead inflates the recorded score. We test the tilt as a prediction for LLM-guided program search, committing each prediction from calibration data before the search runs. Runs from different starts had not reached a common level, so whether the tilt describes the stationary law of LLM editing remains open. 2611.09476v1 by Mingkai Li, Jiajun Wu, Yihan Mao, Zilin Sang, Taosong Yu, Yuan Ma, Minghe Liu, Qianqian Yang, Zhiguo Shi

Large language models have expanded beyond text to vision, audio, and other modalities, but understanding raw electromagnetic (EM) signals remains challenging. Wideband recordings can contain millions of samples, whereas existing waveform-based EM models are primarily evaluated on short in-phase and quadrature (IQ) segments, leaving long-recording understanding and detailed description insufficiently explored. We introduce LEMMO, a large EM model for instruction-driven recognition and description of long raw IQ recordings. LEMMO combines a linear-attention EM encoder with anti-aliased temporal resampling to process up to eight million complex samples. Its EM-text interaction module conditions learnable queries on instructions before retrieving signal evidence. Compact queries support recognition, while structured queries with auxiliary factual supervision organize scene-level properties, signal attributes, and cross-signal relationships for detailed description. We also construct and release a unified 1.38-TB corpus of real-world and synthetic EM signals, including LEMMO-ShortQA and LEMMO-LongDesc. Compared with MERLIN-LoRA, LEMMO improves mean EM-Bench perception accuracy by 15.77 %, mean strategy-generation ROUGE-L F1 by 4.38 points, and mean LEMMO-ShortQA accuracy by 14.31 %. It also improves categorical and numerical fact F1 on both LEMMO-LongDesc tasks while preserving its language backbone's MMLU performance. Our code and datasets are available at https://github.com/N1rv4n4/LEMMO and https://huggingface.co/datasets/DaftJun/RFLAS, respectively.

##### **ENGRAM: From Agent Experience to Weights through Verified Consolidation**
2611.09472v1 by Ruiyi Yang, Zechen Li, Lihuan Li, Du Yin, Hao Xue, Flora D. Salim

Language-model agents use experience through external retrieval and parameter learning. Consolidating stored experience into model weights requires deciding both which experience to train on and when its external records can leave retrieval. Admission based solely on student mastery can exclude useful teacher demonstrations, while removal immediately after training can discard guidance that the model still needs. We introduce ENGRAM, a framework that separates training admission from memory release. A demonstration-aware gate admits skills based on student mastery or sufficient successful teacher demonstrations. The student learns from selected action traces with context from an Engram Trace Graph (ETG). A verification gate then compares the updated model without episodic retrieval against the base model with retrieval on reserved probes. Release requires valid training participation and scores meeting both relative and absolute criteria; records for skills that fail verification remain retrievable. Experiments across interactive and reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over frozen-weight ETG controls, with ablations supporting demonstration-aware admission and selective adapter activation. A controlled held-out comparison shows that verified release substantially reduces the retrievable footprint while remaining close to retain-all performance and outperforming immediate and budget-matched random release. ENGRAM also improves task performance while reducing input-token usage and model calls. Skill-level verification further reduces retrievable experience through selective archival, retaining external guidance for skills that do not meet the release criteria. Code is available in anonymous.4open.science/r/engram-anonymous.

##### **Definitions, Label Names, or Demonstrations? Auditing What Governs LLM Annotation**
2611.09457v1 by Viraj Mishra, Shubham Gupta, Dhruv Gupta, Yuehong Cassandra Tai, Qunhua Li

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to annotate social-science texts, but high accuracy does not establish that they apply researcher-supplied category definitions: predictions may instead reflect prior associations with category labels or labeled demonstrations. We introduce a counterfactual audit that reassigns definitions among output labels, distinguishing adherence to supplied definitions from preservation of a dataset's original mapping. Across three political-text datasets, models rarely follow reassigned definitions, and standard fine-tuning strengthens the original mapping without reliably increasing definition adherence. We then study retrieval-augmented annotation. Retrieving the nearest training examples generally outperforms retrieving examples separately within each category despite using fewer demonstrations. On the two tasks with substantial gains, direct \(k\)-nearest-neighbor classification recovers 74--93\% of the improvement, indicating that neighborhood labels provide much of the predictive signal. Relabeling fixed demonstrations redirects predictions, whereas aligning demonstrations with reassigned definitions substantially increases adherence to the new mapping. Thus, accuracy-improving interventions need not increase codebook adherence; definitions, category labels, demonstrations, retrieval, and training data should be aligned and validated jointly.

##### **From LoRA to Language: Recovering Skill Documents from Adapters**
2611.09454v1 by Saranya Dintakurthy Swadha, Pavan C Shekar, Arpit Jain, Aswanth Krishnan

Agent skills are short documents that teach a language model how to handle a class of tasks. To save context, these documents are increasingly compiled into low-rank adapters (LoRA), which keep the behaviour but lose the text: an adapter is hard to read, edit, audit or move to another model. We ask whether the skill document can be written back from the adapter and its frozen base model, and we score each recovered document by the share of the original's key instructions it states and by the tasks it solves. Reading the weights alone recovered little with the methods we tried; a learned map from weights to text passed on about one bit of the document. Running the adapter worked much better. The Executing Writer, a small writer trained on top of the adapted model, recovers 39-44% of the key instructions of held-out skills, against 1.3-1.5% with another skill's adapter, and on adapters fitted to 41 external skills its documents restore 85-91% of the original's task gain. These strongest results are on adapters whose training stored the document; adapters trained on behaviour alone are much harder for trained writers (1-2% of instructions). For these adapters we test an agent that runs the base and adapted models and writes a skill from where they differ. On ALFWorld its skills recover 58-71% of a LoRA's gain and carry over to models that cannot load the LoRA, lifting Llama-3.1-8B from 5% to 55%. Whether such a skill comes from the adapter depends on what the adapter learned: when its behaviour cannot be guessed, the adapter is the source; when it can, a reward loop without the adapter does as well.

##### **Not All Entropy Is Equal: Reward-Conditioned Entropy Allocation in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards**
2611.09448v1 by Song Yu, Li Li, Zhenming Wu

Policy entropy is widely used to diagnose exploration and collapse in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), yet its magnitude does not consistently track reasoning performance across optimization objectives. We argue that this ambiguity arises because global token entropy conflates uncertainty in successful and failed trajectories, diversity across correct solution strategies, and variation among realizations of the same strategy. We introduce \textbf{Reward-Conditioned Entropy Allocation (RCEA)}, a hierarchical diagnostic framework that separates complete-trajectory entropy from average token entropy, conditions uncertainty on verifier outcome and process validity, measures correct-solution-mode retention, and connects these population-level quantities to local covariance-driven updates. In controlled sweeps over six RLVR objectives at two model scales, entropy trajectories do not induce a stable ordering of reasoning accuracy. Token entropy is higher for verifier-failed than successful trajectories under both broad separately aggregated estimates and a common-question paired analysis, whose confidence intervals exclude equality at 10 of 12 displayed endpoints. A local covariance approximation predicts the direction of adjacent-checkpoint entropy change in 95.5\% of nonfailed transitions; a reward-stratified endpoint proxy further shows that positive chosen-token covariance is more tail-concentrated within failed prefixes. Every reportable trained endpoint exhibits some contraction in independently audited correct-solution-mode breadth, whose positive association with large-\(k\) coverage remains exploratory. These results show that entropy collapse should be diagnosed by which uncertainty is removed, rather than by global entropy alone.

##### **Logit Laundering: Evading Data-Use Auditing in Large Language Models**
2611.09446v1 by Ruihan Hu, Wei Luo, Yu-Ming Shang, Jiakai Wang, Chuxuan Zhang, Mei Li, Xi Zhang, Meikang Qiu

Data-use auditing in Large Language Models (LLMs) aims to determine whether the given data has been used for training, thereby supporting data ownership safeguards. A dominant solution is data watermarking, which embeds traceable signals into training data and detects them in model outputs. Recent studies have proposed many watermarking-based auditing methods in LLMs. However, systematic investigation from an adversarial perspective remains limited: non-compliant LLM providers would use unauthorized copyrighted datasets for training their own models to enhance model capabilities, while attempting to evade detection by auditors, thereby avoiding legal liability. To fill this research gap, we propose a novel evasion framework, termed Logit Laundering, where the non-compliant LLM provider manipulates model outputs at inference time to undermine detectable watermark signals. Specifically, we design a logit reshaping module to suppress watermark-induced statistical signals by integrating a statistical bias estimator as a plug-and-play component. This design keeps the model parameters unchanged and performs manipulation only in the logit space, thereby enabling auditing evasion while minimizing the impact on model's learned knowledge. To further balance evasion effectiveness and model utility, we introduce a confidence-aware scaling module to adaptively calibrate the suppression strength based on next-token prediction confidence. Extensive evaluations show that Logit Laundering can successfully evade existing various state-of-the-art auditing methods, significantly attenuating detectable signals, while preserving model utility on downstream tasks. Our work highlights the need for exploring more robust auditing methods to guard against evasion behaviors.

##### **Typed-Centrality Grounding for Agentic GraphRAG: A Symbolic Approach for Agentic Reasoning**
2611.09414v1 by Rosario Napoli, Antonio Celesti, Massimo Villari, Maria Fazio

Knowledge Graphs (KGs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) represent two approaches for knowledge representation and reasoning over heterogeneous data. Recent research has explored their integration through Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) systems, which use KGs to provide structured context to LLMs. However, existing GraphRAG approaches treat KGs as homogeneous graphs and rely on textual similarity between the input prompt and the context, rather than using it as a source of deterministic knowledge.
  This work proposes an agent-based GraphRAG system that introduces a symbolic layer to integrate deterministic reasoning alongside text similarities. This approach is based on the definition of typed centralities for KGs, where centrality measures are computed separately for each specific KG's class. This information is used to construct a symbolic representation that is provided to the LLM as a deterministic source. The proposed system was mathematically validated and evaluated on a real-world POLE (Person, Object, Location, Event) dataset, comparing reasoning and information retrieval with and without the symbolic layer. The results indicate that the our approach leads to responses that are more consistent with the context, improving the generated outputs.

##### **SmartBloch-Agent: An Agentic Framework for Automated MRI Sequence Design and Optimization**
2611.09413v1 by Jiakai Yang, Haoye Zheng, Yixiao Yue, Qinqin Yang, Zhong Chen, Shuhui Cai, Congbo Cai

Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) pulse sequences organize radiofrequency excitation, gradient encoding, and signal acquisition, determining magnetization evolution and image formation. Sequence research requires translating imaging objectives into executable protocols meeting physical and acquisition constraints, refined via physical simulation, signal reconstruction, and scientific evaluation. Sequence construction, physical verification, parameter search, and assessment involve many tools and manual decisions, limiting automation from objective to verifiable sequence. We develop SmartBloch-Agent, an integrated framework for automated MRI sequence design and optimization. Given objectives and scientific constraints in natural language, an AI agent interprets goals, plans tasks, orchestrates tools, and makes feedback-informed decisions, drawing on a construction module and optimizers for structural design and parameter search. Our GPU-accelerated SmartBloch engine supplies physical computation for both stages, so the framework configures, per sequence and objective, event validation, Full-Bloch magnetization evolution, signal acquisition, image reconstruction, and scientific evaluation, and closes the loop without external MRI simulators. Results return to the agent to guide sequence revision, parameter optimization, and candidate selection, and the design files carry simulation, reconstruction, and evaluation evidence, forming an output package for a real-scanner workflow. Experiments show the framework automatically designs and optimizes MRI sequences for varied objectives and constraints, refines candidates with physical feedback, and improves several parameter-estimation metrics and downstream quantitative reconstruction. SmartBloch-Agent unifies objectives, design, physical execution, and evaluation into a continuous workflow for end-to-end automation of MRI sequence research.

##### **MIRA: Mitigating Pole Geometry Bias in Recurrent Language Models**
2611.09412v1 by Róisín Luo, Ihsan Ullah, Christian Gagné, Dong Li, Karyn Morrissey

We show that the Transformer's recurrent successors --- such as selective state-space models (SSMs), Receptance Weighted Key Value (RWKV), and Retentive Network (RetNet) --- can be characterized as infinite-impulse-response (IIR) systems. The zero--pole geometries of these IIR systems characterize the temporal modes they can represent. However, to trade parallelism, the algebraic structures of the parameterizations of these recurrent systems often give rise to poles constrained to the real axis, thereby inducing an architectural bias in their recurrent dynamics. To unify these recurrent architectures and eliminate their pole geometry bias, inspired by the lattice--ladder realization of general IIR systems, we present \textsc{Mira} (\textbf{M}odal \textbf{I}IR \textbf{R}ecurrent \textbf{A}rchitecture), a unified family of linear-time recurrent architectures for building large language models (LLMs) with a highly parallel algebraic structure. Each \textsc{Mira} block realizes a higher-order IIR system that represents temporal modes from token streams through a lattice--ladder structure, followed by a feed-forward network (FFN). This realization enables the model to represent the temporal modes associated with both real and complex-conjugate poles. We pretrain \textsc{Mira-S}, a 12-layer small-scale LLM with 0.14B parameters composed entirely of \textsc{Mira} blocks. Throughout the pretraining, \textsc{Mira-S} achieves lower perplexity than strong recurrent baselines, exhibits stronger context-window generalization beyond the training length, and attains higher throughput with a dedicated CUDA kernel.

##### **DataHelix: A CPU-GPU Co-Scheduling Data Plane for Multimodal Model Training**
2611.09411v1 by Yuanhe Li, Jiazhen Wu, Dian Ding, Guangtao Xue, Yiming Zhang

Multimodal model training breaks the loose coupling between data loading and model execution assumed by conventional training pipelines. Large per-sample variation in decoding cost, token/latent count, and tensor size causes both CPU preprocessing time and GPU compute time to vary substantially across data-parallel ranks (training processes). In synchronous training, these two sources of heterogeneity interact tightly: GPU execution determines how long the input pipeline has to prepare the next batch, while delayed preprocessing in any rank in turn stalls all GPUs at synchronization. As a result, optimizing CPU throughput or GPU load balance in isolation cannot eliminate pipeline bubbles. We present DataHelix, a metadata-first CPU-GPU co-scheduling data plane for multimodal training. DataHelix jointly reasons about preprocessing demand and GPU execution progress, treating each GPU compute window as the readiness budget for the next batch and coordinating preprocessing across ranks to minimize deadline misses rather than CPU makespan. To avoid expensive data movement, placement is decided while samples remain lightweight descriptors, before decoding and collation amplify them into large tensors. Descriptor-level token-latent-aware batching performs coarse pre-balancing, while cost-gated collective rebalancing corrects residual skew only when its expected benefit exceeds transfer cost. Across 11 video and multimodal workloads on 8 Ascend 910B accelerators, DataHelix reduces mean deadline-miss rate by 67.8% and mean p95 input-wait latency by 87.0%, while achieving 2.72x geometric-mean throughput over rank-local DataLoaders and 1.82x over a Ray actor pool under the same CPU budget.
