##### **Beyond the principle-practice gap: Responsible AI as situated epistemic and ethical practice**
2611.06709v1 by Thao Do Ngoc, Emma Carmel

Responsible AI scholarship has found that ethical principles are frequently not reflected in AI design and development. Conceptualised as a `gap' between principle and practice, this is framed as a problem of implementation, awareness or compliance, and by implication, a deficit of systems or individual behaviour. This paper sets out an alternative explanation. Drawing on fourteen semi-structured interviews with actors designing and governing AI-enabled anti-trafficking systems across law enforcement, commercial, research, and survivor-support contexts, it argues that such apparent gaps emerge from the varied and complex institutional and professional conditions through which actors come to recognise some issues, but not others, as ethical problems requiring action. We show that epistemic choices are intertwined with ethical judgements, and trace four interconnected moments---perception, judgement, situated action and socio-technical materialisation---through which concerns become intelligible, are weighed and acted upon, and are embedded in technical artefacts and governance arrangements. Actors often hold genuine ethical commitments, but these commitments are shaped by institutional logics and professional moral economies: some concerns are materialised, while others are narrowed, deferred or left unrealised. We show that ethical agency is exercised within and between competing constraints of profession, role, prior experience, and institutional settings. The paper argues that responsible AI governance requires purposeful and well-developed epistemic infrastructures that can cut across these constraints to establish institutional conditions through which the knowledge of affected communities and potential harms becomes receivable and actionable in design.

Human Motion Prediction (HMP) aims to predict future human poses at different moments according to observed past motion sequences. Previous approaches mainly treated the prediction of different temporal moments as a single prediction task and learned the predictions of varied moments simultaneously, which would encounter a main limitation: the learning of short-term predictions (referring to ``near-future'' prediction) could be hindered by the predictions of long-term (referring to ``far-future'' prediction) motions. In this paper, we develop a novel temporal continual learning framework called Continual Prior Compensation (CPC) to progressively train HMP models, in which we divide the prediction task of motions corresponding to varied temporal moments into several subtasks and train the model in a multi-stage manner. To mitigate the prior information forgetting in the progressive training, we further introduce a learnable random variable Prior Compensation Factor (PCF) to explicitly measure the prior knowledge loss. We theoretically show that the PCF can be efficiently learned together with the model parameters by minimizing a reasonable upper bound of the objective function. The proposed CPC is further enhanced to estimate the prior information loss for each subtask and a new framework called Continual Prior Compensation++ (CPC++) with Fine-Grained Prior Compensation Factor (FGPCF) is finally developed. Our CPC and CPC++ frameworks are quite flexible and can be easily integrated with different HMP backbone models and adapted to various datasets and applications. Extensive experiments on three HMP benchmark datasets using multiple SOTA HMP backbones (PGBIG, siMLPe, MotionMixer, and LTD) demonstrate the effectiveness and flexibility of our frameworks.

##### **ArcRAG: Agentic Compositional Retrieval over Knowledge Graphs**

Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (GraphRAG) answers questions by retrieving over a knowledge graph built from a corpus. Across the two constructions, we find that retrieval need not be co-designed with the graph: query-time interface induction keeps a construction-agnostic retriever effective when the construction changes, although construction still shapes absolute accuracy.

#### Abstracts
##### **CPU-ARC: Agentic Reasoning Caching for on-CPU Reasoning Reuse**
2611.09891v1 by Peter Belcak, Greg Heinrich, Saurav Muralidharan, Shizhe Diao, Pavlo Molchanov

Large language models reinvent the wheel all the time. They conflate task-solving with the execution of the solutions, which forces them to solve every problem from scratch. We show that once a task has been reasoned through, the application of its solution to a new problem instance can be done very efficiently and with little involvement from language models. We introduce agentic reasoning caching, a method for constructing robust CPU-executable agentic reasoning caches (ARCs) from LLM reasoning traces. ARCs show on-par performance with frontier LLMs across a range of difficult mathematical and scientific tasks, are fully explainable, continually trainable, robust to input perturbations, and 16-291x cheaper to invoke than the source model.

摘要：

##### **Can Agentic Systems Close the Gap in Radiology with Production AI Tools?**
2611.09836v1 by Aubin Charley, Julien Khlaut, Charles Corbière, Pierre Manceron, Corentin Dancette, Léo Machado

Radiology AI has developed along two paths: specialized models that perform well on narrow tasks but cover few clinical findings, and generalist models that address a broader range of tasks but remain prone to hallucination and inaccurate measurements. Combining the two through agentic systems offers a route towards comprehensive radiology reporting, but published systems are tied to one modality or body part. We describe a radiology agentic framework that standardizes how an orchestrator calls modular tools to produce a structured report, and use it to measure what such a system can do with the tools from FDA-cleared radiology AI devices. Report generation is simulated on a large multi-modality report corpus, with the orchestrator given either a catalog of finding classes consolidated from the cleared devices, each available on the modalities it is cleared for, or an unlimited catalog in which any class can be requested, and planning either from the clinical indication or from the target report. Tool outputs are simulated from the reference report, so the comparison isolates tool availability and orchestration from tool error. With the cleared catalog and the indication alone, the orchestrator recovers under a third of the clinically actionable findings; recovery improves substantially with the unlimited catalog, and further when the plan is written from the target report. Attributing every missed finding to a cause shows that most are classes no cleared device offers for the study's modality, and that a third are classes or tools that were offered and not called. Adding the most frequently missing tools closes most of the gap to the unlimited catalog on held-out reports, whereas a smaller orchestrator with the unlimited catalog gives up the whole gain. Tool availability and effective orchestration are thus complementary constraints on comprehensive AI-assisted radiology reporting.

摘要：

##### **Agentic AI for Healthcare Claim Denial Analysis: A Policy-Grounded Framework for Privacy Preservation**
2611.09423v1 by Sofia Campos, Carlos Martins, Pedro Pires, João Gama, Bruno Veloso, Pedro Santos

In the healthcare sector, claim denials impose significant financial and administrative burdens, emphasizing the need for automated and trustworthy decision-support systems. This paper proposes a modular Agentic Artificial Intelligence (AI) framework for healthcare claim denial analysis, integrating specialized agents for denial risk prediction, risk factor identification, diagnosis-procedure-medication code relationship analysis, and counterfactual analysis. The framework integrates predictive analytics, graph-based learning, a Hybrid Contextual Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipeline grounded in healthcare policy documents, and a dedicated HIPAA Compliance Agent to support privacy protection. Evaluated on a synthetic healthcare claims dataset, the framework achieved 98% traceability of reported numerical findings, validated 214 of 216 policy citations, and removed all 280 identified sensitive identifiers without modifying the underlying analysis. These findings pave the way for further research into Agentic AI as a framework for explainable, policy-aware, and privacy-preserving healthcare claim denial management.

摘要：

##### **Can LLMs Debug Robots by Editing Their Weights?**
2611.09376v1 by Jusheng Zhang, Keze Wang, Liang Lin

A language model can repair a robot policy's lexical instruction grounding by editing just 65 token-embedding rows. We bring weight-space programming to robot foundation policies: GPT-6 Astra reads weights and uses a CPU workspace and budgeted simulator feedback, without gradient updates or new demonstrations. Three independent sessions on $π_{0.5}$ all improve success on 765 held-out paraphrases; the best raises success from 86.3\% to 92.0\% (48 repairs, four regressions; exact paired $p=1.31\times10^{-10}$). Among the 335 instructions containing edited tokens, success rises from 74.3\% to 87.5\%; the other 430 episodes have zero outcome changes. Matched random-direction edits do not help. Full replacement using the editor's own lexicon explains much of the gain but produces more regressions; its direct difference from the editor is not significant at 0.05. Across 13 further robot sessions, the editor declines 12 candidates; the sole retained edit does not show a significant held-out gain. Separately, benchmark metadata lowers FastWAM success from 70.0\% to 48.6\%, while the evaluated VLA's aggregate rate is unchanged. The evidence supports precise lexical repair, not general acquisition of missing robot capabilities.

摘要：

##### **AutoRefine: Auto-Calibrating Code Judges from Workflow Outcomes**
2611.09291v1 by Shervin Shahidi, James Poon, Hang Xie, Kenneth O. Xu, Robin Håkanson, Vikram Nair, Jens Pillgram-Larsen, Nachiappan Nagappan

LLM judges increasingly serve as quality gates for code changes that have no test oracle, but a judge prompt must be calibrated for its task and recalibrated as the system it judges evolves, while hand labels for the calibration are resource intensive and go stale. Development workflows emit outcome labels for free (tests pass or fail, changes land or are abandoned), but these labels are noisy. We present AutoRefine, a loop that rewrites a deployable judge's prompt to reproduce outcome labels, with an informed teacher that sees the judge's input plus the outcome, explains each label it keeps, and abstains when the outcome cannot be explained from what the judge sees. On a held-out dataset with an execution oracle, calibration raised chance-corrected agreement (Scott's $π$) from 0.36 to 0.60. On a deployed agent's outcome labels, with 25\% flipped at random to introduce noise, the teacher set aside flipped \textsc{fail} labels at more than twice the rate of genuine ones, cutting overall label noise from 25\% to 20\%. Recalibrating that agent's judge using AutoRefine raised its agreement with merge/abandon outcomes on 17 independently held-out diffs from the current workflow ($π$ 0.16 to 0.51); on 16 diffs from older workflows, the previous judge stayed ahead, illustrating the need for recalibration as the agent evolves. By abstaining, the teacher not only removes noisy labels but also flags changes whose outcome the judge's input cannot explain, telling a team when a judge needs better inputs rather than a better prompt.

摘要：

##### **PHISHREV+: An ASP-Based Post-Hoc Defeasible Machine Learning Framework for Phishing Detection**
2611.09273v1 by Mainak Sen, Kumar Sanka Ray, Amlan Chakrabarti

Phishing website detection remains a critical cybersecurity challenge. Although machine learning classifiers achieve high accuracy on benchmark datasets, they suffer from a fundamental limitation: once trained, their decision boundaries are fixed and cannot incorporate new contextual evidence without retraining the model. This work presents \textbf{PHISHREV+}, a hybrid framework that instantiates the notion of \textit{post-hoc defeasible machine learning}. This framework does not replace the classifier. It treats the initial classifier predictions as defeasible beliefs and revises them through an Answer Set Programming based non-monotonic reasoning(NMR) layer. The framework operates in two phases. In the machine learning phase, four classifiers (SVM, KNN, DT and RF) are trained on the 87 feature benchmark dataset. In the post-hoc NMR phase, four defeasible rules derived from exploratory data analysis of the training set, and validated through Chi-square and Mann-Whitney U tests, formally revise initial predictions. The proposed framework achieves consistent false positive reduction across all our classifiers SVM (41$\rightarrow$39), KNN (47$\rightarrow$46), DT (62$\rightarrow$61), and RF (52$\rightarrow$51) while maintaining complete false negative parity with zero increase overhead. A comprehensive ablation studies across seven rule configurations confirms that the hierarchical combination of all four defeasible rules is necessary to achieve this optimal balance. Instance level explainability is provided through eXplainable ASP (XASP), which generates formal derivation graphs showing which defeasible rules fired and why the initial belief was retracted, a capability absent from all existing phishing detection approaches.

摘要：

##### **RAG-Enhanced LLMs for Interactive Explainability in Clinical Decision Support Systems**
2611.09232v1 by Lorenzo Mannocci, Francesca Naretto, Lucia Passaro, Anna Monreale

Artificial Intelligence (AI) is increasingly embedded in high-risks decision-making systems across domains such as healthcare, finance, and the judicial system. As reliance on predictive models grows, so does the demand for Explainable AI (XAI) to ensure transparency, trust, and interpretability. However, existing XAI approaches often fail to present explanations in a format accessible to domain experts, limiting their practical utility. Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently emerged as a bridge between complex models and end-users, offering natural language explanations. Yet, their use introduces new risks, including hallucinated outputs and lack of source traceability. To address these challenges, we propose a Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS) that not only combines standard predictive models with XAI techniques but also incorporates a Retrieval-Augmented Generation module as an interactive enhancer. This module grounds explanations in verified medical knowledge, reformulates technical outputs into a human-like format, and enables users-such as clinicians-to actively query the system. By fostering an interactive, human-in-the-loop environment, our approach empowers domain experts to explore model decisions, contextualize explanations, and build trust in AI-assisted diagnostics. The study concludes with preliminary experiments validating the proposed methodology.

摘要：

##### **Learning When to Think: Dual-Reference Offline Optimization for Adaptive VLM Reasoning**
2611.09154v1 by Hongbin Lin, Sizhe Zou, Juangui Xu, Xinyue Xu, Zhuoli Ouyang, Stanislav Kolchin, Chengwei Qin, Zhongxiang Dai, Yao Shu

Reasoning traces improve vision-language models on complex multimodal tasks, but forcing every input to follow a long reasoning process leads to overthinking and unnecessary inference cost. We study adaptive fast/slow thinking for VLMs, where one model answers directly for simple inputs and reasons step by step when needed. Fast answers and slow traces often come from distinct behavior policies, making a single-reference offline objective misweight one mode. We propose DUAL-REFERENCE BOLT (DR-BOLT), which distinguishes the desired fast/slow mixture reference from the sampling distribution and corrects their mismatch in reward-weighted training. Fast/slow partition competition explains routing in the optimal target; an efficient source-wise correction approximates the exact objective when response formats are well separated. Matched Qwen3-VL comparisons use identical candidate banks to isolate the objective from slow-teacher quality. DR-BOLT improves score-length trade-offs over the evaluated offline and online baselines on ScienceQA and three held-out transfer benchmarks, with a 24.1% cross-benchmark slow ratio and a 2.90-fold end-to-end training speedup over reproduced GRPO. The first two tasks measure how well a model's similarity score predicts the proportion of listeners who respond "same" and the majority response. We evaluate eleven publicly available speech models. Models trained with speaker labels align with listeners better than self-supervised models do, yet a model with a 12% error rate on speaker labels aligns with listeners as well as models with error rates below 2%. VIPBench enables the evaluation of speaker models against human perception on real, cloned, and morphed speech, and our results motivate training speaker models directly on listener judgments.

##### **Personas Are Not People: Identity as State, Not Attribute**
2611.05292v1 by Tamunotonye Harry, Yuanyuan Feng

Synthetic populations, large sets of simulated people, are increasingly used in place of real people to pretest surveys, simulate users and communities, and evaluate AI systems. Each simulated person is defined by a persona, a written profile of fixed traits, demographics, and attitudes that a language model is instructed to act out. Every such system assumes that a person's identity is a stable attribute that behaves the same way in every situation the simulation presents. We show that this assumption is not a harmless simplification. Three decades of personality research find the opposite. How a given person behaves varies more across contexts than people vary from one another. Drawing on identity economics, which describes how different situations activate different identities and norms within the same person, we separate behavior into three parts, which are stable traits, the identity a context calls up, and the situation itself. We then prove, under two stated assumptions, that any simulation built on fixed personas, no matter how detailed the personas or how large the underlying model, cannot reproduce the part of a population's behavior that is specific to it and its circumstances. We propose a new evaluation criterion based on this result and a person-in-context design that can meet it in principle. We test one version on a naturalistic Reddit personality corpus and on a published clinical-trial recruitment survey of a community with documented distrust of medical institutions. The design does produce context-dependent behavior when tested directly, but in neither setting does it fully match the real population, and we show where it falls short. The consequence for practice is a risk that synthetic-population evaluations misrepresent the groups whose behavior is most shaped by context, a distortion that richer personas cannot correct.

Repeated interaction increases receptiveness without eliminating this ordering; expertise also matters, while rhetorical differences are smaller. The pattern appears across multiple models, but is not fixed: fine-tuning on different political corpora can largely erase or reverse it. These results show that LLMs can respond systematically differently to participants in the same political debate, making such interactional asymmetries important for understanding their emerging role in online public discourse.

##### **Spectral Degeneration of Self-Attention under Isotropic Score Geometry**
2611.02778v1 by Mengda Li, Jianfeng Yao

We study the spectral structure of the self-attention matrix in Transformer models through an idealized spectral model for the pre-softmax score matrix. ##### **When Prediction Shapes the Future: A BlueSky Agenda for Reflexive Time Series Analysis**
2611.02511v1 by Tao Xie, Yiqun Zhang, Yiu-ming Cheung

Time-series models are increasingly deployed in operational systems (e.g., inventory management and clinical care) where predictions trigger interventions that alter future observations, producing records we term AI-shaped trajectories. This sequence-prediction coupling deepens as such systems evolve into agents that autonomously predict, act, and remember across long horizons. Such AI-shaped trajectories support immediate performance monitoring, but turn ambiguous once reused as passive data. An algorithm may then misread a prediction-prevented stockout as naturally stable demand, or an averted medical crisis as a false alarm. This ambiguity then propagates into everything built on the record, from retraining and agent memory to downstream predictive tasks. As a result, systems may internalize these intervention effects as natural phenomena, drifting further from the truth at every closed-loop cycle. In this Blue Sky paper, we advocate an agenda for reflexive time-series analysis that shifts the analytical object from the intervention-agnostic sequence to the prediction-action-observation loop. The agenda requires closed-loop provenance to track the operational context (e.g., predictions, policies, and actions), sequence identification to estimate how those actions affect future values under explicit causal assumptions, and closed-loop evaluation to separate intrinsic process prediction from policy-driven effects. This foundation is essential for integrating autonomous agents into such loops at scale, enabling the joint learning of intervention effects and intrinsic dynamics for robust temporal representations.

##### **Machine Learning Beyond Statistical Consistency for Hybrid AI**
2611.01914v1 by Jinsook Kim, Jinho Kang

We propose a new definition of machine learning, according to which a machine learns a function when it computes the function successfully, that is, when the value it returns for each input has a proper derivation in a formal system. We extend Gödel's Representability Theorem, which equates computation with derivation, to empirical targets in two ways: the theorem is extended to the computable reals, and the data are admitted into the system as empirical definitions that record what has been observed. We prove that a machine learns a true probability if and only if two conditions are met. The Truth Condition is that its estimate converges to the true probability, which is statistical consistency. The Assurance Condition is that the machine properly derives its own convergence inside the system. Learning thus lies beyond statistical consistency, for it requires a derivation of the convergence from the record. A global assumption about the data, such as the i.i.d. assumption, does not supply such a derivation, because it leads only to a circular one, as we show for PAC learning. On the other hand, the two conditions are met for the probability of a sentence over a finite ideal corpus. A real machine can approximate a true probability only if an ideal machine can learn it precisely, that is, only in a domain that meets the two conditions. Modern AI works in such a domain, and we offer this as a clue to its success. Modern AI is hybrid as well, in two senses: First, it performs two kinds of tasks, namely probabilistic ones such as predicting the next word and deductive ones such as proving and coding. Second, it combines two parts of different kinds, a statistical proposer and a deductive verifier, for proving and coding. Our framework explains both senses of hybridity in one formal system.

##### **Special and General Computational Aesthetics**

Computational aesthetics is a field at the intersection of computer science, artificial intelligence, aesthetics, and other disciplines. Without distinguishing scope, objectives, and methods, it may be narrowed to image aesthetic quality assessment or expanded into an umbrella term for AI's involvement in art.
  We analyze its internal structure and its relations with neighboring concepts, and propose a two-level scope: special computational aesthetics (SCA) and general computational aesthetics (GCA). SCA centers on aesthetic measurement and aesthetic process modeling. The former primarily corresponds to "Aesthetic Perception": computationally identifying and representing aesthetic attributes and predicting human aesthetic evaluations; the latter primarily to "Aesthetic Understanding": computationally describing, testing, and explaining how human aesthetic perception and judgment are formed. GCA extends this core to include "Aesthetic Generation," namely artistic generation guided by computational aesthetic models. Some computational aesthetic tasks can also use non-AI methods. Research in this scope using AI algorithms and models we define as AI aesthetics, which may involve the special core and the general extension. "Aesthetic Generation" intersects with AIGC, AI art, and computational creativity, but belongs to the general extension defined here only when computational aesthetic models substantively constrain, guide, or optimize artwork generation. We also draw on Feng Peng's discussion of the computability of beauty, aesthetic experience, and art as a theoretical reference for clarifying boundaries between computational aesthetics and related concepts.

##### **Meta-memorization and memorization scaling laws in transformers**
2611.01564v1 by Alex Nguyen, Kenneth A. Norman, Gautam Reddy

Larger transformers can memorize more data, with a capacity (in bits) that scales roughly linearly with the number of parameters. Counterintuitively, larger models also memorize faster, requiring fewer repetitions of a sequence during training before it is memorized. We reproduce both memorization scaling laws in transformers trained on random binary sequences, showing that rapid memorization can arise even when data contain no semantic or compositional structure. Rapid memorization is due to a meta-memorization effect, where models trained to memorize a large set of random sequences learn to memorize novel sequences more quickly. The rate $r(t)$ at which models memorize grows with training time $t$ as a power-law $r(t) \sim t^β$ with a fixed exponent $β$ but an increasing upper cutoff across model scales. Meta-memorization enables even one-layer transformers to few-shot memorize a 100-bit sequence. This effect is partly explained by the formation of structured attention heads that convey position-invariant sequence information to MLPs where memorized sequences are stored. Meta-memorization is recapitulated in a simplified model of a dense associative memory where retrieval is constrained by an attentional bottleneck. Our results suggest transformers, through pre-training, acquire reusable mechanisms that enable highly efficient storage and retrieval of memories.

Fourth, a common mechanism explains the patterns above: writer preference and self-preference trace to a combination of how much each writer leans on specific stylistic cues and how strongly evaluators, often unanimously, reward or penalise those same cues. A further check finds little evidence that an unmeasured "writer fingerprint" remains once these same features are accounted for. We discuss implications for algorithmic fairness in employment, regulation of AI-assisted screening, and the design of auditing frameworks that can be reapplied as the model landscape evolves.

Good decisions often require considering how outcomes will feel. At every epoch the current encoder retrieves chains for the training questions; each state it visits, including those built on its own mistakes, is labeled with the earliest gold passage still missing while the other missing gold passages are masked, so that no correct continuation is penalized; and the encoder is updated contrastively on these states mixed with teacher-forced ones, so that its training distribution follows it as it learns. Another route to faster progress is to expand the resources brought to bear through knowledge sharing and coordination across society, even while comparable advances require increasing work. Grasp also applies to collectives, which may outperform their members yet require more total work than one system with their combined resources. Establishing and sustaining organisation can itself be deep even when the primary task is simple. These demands may help explain the richness of biological and social systems. In agent collectives, this organisational work can generate goals beyond assigned tasks, including maintaining the collective itself. Verifying numerical claims in research written by AI agents is an important but challenging problem. Recent benchmarks show that many agent-written papers fail integrity checks, but such verdicts, often produced by a single LLM judge, identify failure without explaining its nature, and existing checks either test a paper for internal consistency or test whether a reported value appears somewhere in an agent's output. We re-analyze all 540 released judgments from a recent public benchmark of AI research agents (nine agents, fifteen open research problems) and manually label 256 numerical claims from 18 submissions. Failures concentrate on alignment between the paper and the underlying work rather than overt cheating, and about four in five unsupported claims correspond to values in the agent's own files that come from the wrong experiment, metric, or run. We introduce a deterministic claim-to-evidence checker that scores whether the surrounding context identifies the claimed result and calibrates each claim against numerical decoys. With its rules fixed before labeling, it certifies claims with over 90% precision against blinded annotators (78 of 85; Cohen's kappa = 0.89), while verifying only a subset of claims; most claims it cannot verify are nevertheless traceable, so failure to verify is not evidence of falsehood. An LLM verifier given the same evidence recovers more traceable claims but accepts about three times as many misattributed ones, so the two are best used together. Applied to 99 submissions, the checker reveals large differences in traceability across agents, and on 30 submissions a second judge configuration, with a different model and harness, orders the agents' vendor groups as the official judge does while passing twice as many papers. These findings motivate provenance-first auditing of AI-produced research.

摘要：

##### **Governance-Aware Federated Learning for Multi-Agent AI Systems: A Policy-Driven Framework for Trustworthy Collaborative AI**
2610.38411v1 by Kiranmayee Janardhan, Karthick S, Madhusmita Panda, Bharathi Vokkaliga Ganesh, Syed Ahmed

Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative model training without exchanging raw data, but conventional FL provides limited runtime governance, evidence verification, fairness assessment, explainability monitoring, and auditability. This paper proposes Governance-Aware Federated Learning (GAFL), which combines independent governance-evidence verification, temporal trust, fairness and explanation assessment, anomaly detection, policy enforcement, normalized aggregation, and tamper-evident audit logging. Client-reported governance values are authenticated, bound to their submitted model updates, and independently recomputed before aggregation. A cross-silo prototype with 10 participants was evaluated on the Adult Income dataset under honest, benign non-IID, biased-data, malicious-update, non-compliant, falsified-evidence, and mixed-threat scenarios. Four methods were compared across five random seeds: FedAvg, FedAvg with anomaly filtering, GAFL without independent verification, and complete GAFL. Complete GAFL intervened on all affected participants at the final round across the evaluated risk scenarios, including identifying 100% of falsified-evidence participants, whereas GAFL without verification identified none of the falsified-evidence participants. Final macro-F1 ranged from 0.7520 to 0.7733 across GAFL scenarios. Complete GAFL required 173.67 plus or minus 26.94 ms of governance processing per round, compared with 3.82 plus or minus 0.79 ms for FedAvg. A strict demographic-parity threshold also caused frequent interventions for honest participants, demonstrating a significant fairness-policy and utility trade-off.

摘要：

##### **A Tutorial on Generative Models for Music Audio**
2610.38252v1 by Mateusz Modrzejewski, Joanna Kurkowska, Dorien Herremans

Music audio generation spans instrumental clips, full songs with vocals, edits of existing recordings, and systems that play alongside a musician in real time. This monograph explains the methods behind these systems for readers familiar with neural-network training who are new to music generation. It introduces neural audio codecs and autoencoders, autoregressive and masked token models, diffusion and flow matching, sampling acceleration, and preference optimization in a shared notation. Worked examples connect training objectives and sampling procedures to published systems. The application sections relate the musical requirements of instrumental generation, songs, editing, and live performance to model design. Comparison tables record representations, methods, inputs, tasks, and release information documented in papers and official sources. Symbolic composition, speech, and sound effects are included where they connect to music audio. We explain how to interpret automatic metrics and learned judges, design and report listening tests, and assess evidence about data provenance, memorization, attribution, and output detection. Selected legal decisions and disputes illustrate the implications for training and release. The reviewed systems combine established generative paradigms with new representations, planning stages, and post-training procedures. Differences in data, scale, and evaluation limit what cross-system comparisons can establish about the sources of reported gains.

摘要：

##### **Precedent or Intent? What Coding Agents Follow When Evolving Existing Code**
2610.38244v1 by Haichao Zhu, Geyang Xu, Zhaorui Yang, Jiyuan Wang, Qian Zhang

Existing code reveals past implementation decisions but rarely explains the reasoning behind them. When coding agents implement new features or modify existing functionality, past decisions may conflict with new task instructions. This raises a fundamental question for agentic software evolution: when multiple implementations satisfy the same functional requirements, how do coding agents decide whether to follow existing code or new task instructions? We investigate this question through bidirectional, controlled experiments in purpose-built repositories, followed by validation in real-world open-source repositories. Our approach isolates three competing sources of guidance: (1) agents' default preferences, (2) repository precedents, and (3) explicit task instructions. We conduct 5,625 trials across four coding agents and six implementation pairs, with validation in four open-source repositories spanning three programming languages. We find that agents share the same default implementation choices, yet overwhelmingly follow repository precedent when it conflicts with those defaults (359/360 trials). Crucially, not all precedents carry equal influence: three supporting local files yield 96% adoption, compared with only 4% for sibling-resource files, and changing file placement alone can shift adoption from 0% to 100% at a fixed example count. When new task intent conflicts with existing code, written priority rules achieve substantially higher success than code examples alone (59/60 versus 38/60 joint successes for two agents on two implementation pairs). The effectiveness of explicit rules also extends to open-source repositories, where a harness-loaded rule raises joint success from 0/120 to 117/120. These findings motivate design-decision traceability that preserves not only implementation choices but also the rationale and priorities that should guide future changes.

摘要：

##### **Disparity Laundering: Consensus Without Fairness in Clinical Multi-Agent Systems**
2610.38215v1 by Xiaoyang Wang, Tianrui Wang, Christopher C. Yang

Clinical large language models are increasingly fielded as multi-agent teams, on the assumption that deliberation averages out individual error. It does not average out bias. Across a full factorial of five model families and four aggregation topologies on 120 real MIMIC-IV emergency cases under counterfactual demographic swaps, no configuration neutralizes the disparity: all 20 cells commit the direction of their agents' independent answers, giving Black patients more acute triage. Every model has a topology that amplifies its baseline gap, but which one varies by model, so no design is reliably safe or reliably amplifying: the disparity is relocated, never removed. Late-stage fixes fail: fairness instructions and demographic masking leave the gap untouched, and per-agent self-debiasing widens it, because the bias is already encoded in the agents' ordinary clinical numbers. We call this disparity laundering; the audits now applied to clinical AI, per model, per output, per version, can certify the parts but never the committed decision. Interventions close the gap exactly when they make counterfactual versions visible inside the system and pool them, through deliberation, into the committed decision. A diverse-perspective ensemble does both while keeping the team, with no retraining or architectural change: the disparity falls by 67 to 94% where all five perspectives pool at every round, and the one topology that never pools them is the one it cannot reliably repair. Medical multi-agent systems do not filter demographic bias; they launder it into plausible decisions no single-version audit can catch, and the only defense is to make the bias visible.

摘要：

##### **The Prestige: Benchmarking Cognitive Visual Reasoning using Magic Tricks**
2610.38103v1 by Shuo Wen, Beixi Du, Edwin Meriaux, Junming Shi, Chloe Si, David Meger, Doina Precup, Gregory Dudek

We introduce The Prestige, a video understanding benchmark that probes cognitive visual reasoning through professional magic performances, where deliberate misdirection challenges models to track events and explain unexpected outcomes. The benchmark comprises open-ended questions ranging from temporal and causal understanding to robustness against adversarial questions. Evaluation of leading open-source and proprietary VLMs reveals a substantial gap between even the strongest model and human performance. We also find systematic discrepancies between human and automated grading and identify recurring model failure modes. Ablations further show that models can select correct multiple-choice answers even when they fail to generate correct explanations independently, motivating further investigations into question formats. Together, these findings highlight limitations in both VLM reasoning and its evaluation, underscoring the need for more reliable video understanding models and more rigorous benchmarks.

摘要：

##### **Are Seismic Foundation Models More Than Glorified Feature Extractors?**
2610.38003v1 by Laura Laurenti, David Liu, Brian Kulis, Men-Andrin Meier, Christopher Johnson

Earthquake sequences are stochastic on two separated timescales: the seismic waves of any individual earthquake propagate over seconds to minutes, while the earthquakes themselves arrive as a sparse, self-exciting occurrence process, with inter-event intervals from seconds to months. Respecting both scales in one training objective is the open challenge. Foundation models (FMs) for seismic waveforms instead import their learning targets from audio and vision, where every target is computed from a single fixed-length window. Such a window holds one realization of the wave propagation (an earthquake trace), never the occurrence process that decides when earthquakes happen. We develop nine model designs sharing one Mamba encoder-decoder, trained on a fixed corpus of 3M windows, varying the self-supervised objective, quantization bottleneck and mixer; the two key comparisons are architecture-matched, varying the objective alone. Each checkpoint is evaluated frozen on three downstream tasks. Varying the target redistributes downstream skill rather than raising it, and no checkpoint wins everywhere. Adding masking pressure to our strongest classifier re-specializes it rather than making it a generalist: it gains 6.9 points of few-shot P-wave picking precision and loses 9.6 points of few-shot classification accuracy. By the standard imported from language and vision, these models are feature extractors rather than FMs. Controls on capacity, architecture, and domain proximity do not explain why skill moves rather than rises. Against task-specific baselines the advantage is regime-dependent: our checkpoints beat them few-shot on classification and ground-motion prediction, and trail them on full data. We argue the binding constraint is the target, and that settling the question requires a learning target defined across events, the timescale that makes seismicity stochastic in the first place.
